## Bug fix: Cabernet image cropped/cut off vertically

Reported live: "the picture of the cabernet sauvignon does not have the same format as the others... it is
cut off vertically." Rather than guess at the composition difference, used the Read tool to actually view
both the Cabernet image and a reference image (Pinot Nero) side by side. Confirmed visually: the other 5
images have generous white space above and below the bottle in their own frame; the Cabernet image crops
tight to the bottle, filling almost the entire 637×1000 canvas top-to-bottom with minimal headroom.

`.collection-showcase__category img` used `object-fit: cover` with a fixed `aspect-ratio: 3/4` box — safe
for the loosely-framed images (cropping only trims empty background), but for the tightly-framed Cabernet
image there's no spare space to crop into, so `cover` cut directly into the bottle (the cap and/or base).

**Fix**: changed `object-fit: cover` to `object-fit: contain` for all 6 images uniformly — not a one-off
patch for just the Cabernet image, since the same underlying cropping risk applies to any future image swap
with different native framing. `contain` guarantees no image can ever be cropped, regardless of its own
proportions, at the cost of some images showing a bit more surrounding space within their button than
others — a much smaller issue than clipping the subject. `aspect-ratio: 3/4` is kept as-is, since that still
usefully defines a consistent button footprint/shape across the row; only how the image content fills that
footprint changed.

Verified: `npm run check`/`build` pass. Confirmed the new `object-fit: contain` value compiled into
`dist/client/styles/site.css` and is being served correctly by a fresh preview server. Full route smoke
test passes. Both the preview process and its `workerd` child were stopped cleanly afterward (applying the
lesson from the note above).

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 18 refinement: trigger the brake on scroll start, not on approach to the section

User asked for the scroll-jacking brake to engage as soon as the user starts scrolling down, rather than
requiring an ordinary scroll through most of the hero + spacer + Task 19's 280px margin before the brake's
old trigger condition (`rect.top < innerHeight`, checking the *target section's* own position) was ever
satisfied. Confirmed understanding and flagged the real tradeoff before implementing, since the change
means the animation now has to cover a much longer distance: presented two options (trigger immediately
with a distance-scaled duration vs. the simpler but less complete fix of just shrinking Task 19's margin).
**User chose triggering immediately.**

`initPhilosophySectionBrake()` in `src/components/Hero.astro`:
- Trigger condition changed from checking `section.getBoundingClientRect()` to a plain
  `window.scrollY > TRIGGER_SCROLL_THRESHOLD_PX` (30px) check — same small-floor pattern already
  established in Task 17's fade trigger, for consistency. The reset condition (which re-arms the brake for
  a future approach) simplified the same way: resets once `scrollY` drops back to ≤30px, i.e. the user has
  scrolled back up near the very top of the page, rather than checking the section's far-away position.
- `engageBrake()`'s slide duration is now `Math.min(SLIDE_DURATION_MAX_MS, SLIDE_DURATION_FLOOR_MS +
  distance * SLIDE_MS_PER_PX)` (floor 400ms, +0.3ms per px of distance, capped at 1200ms) instead of a
  fixed 600ms — since the brake can now engage right after leaving the hero, the distance to the centered
  target is often much larger than before (previously a short final approach); a fixed short duration over
  that much longer distance would have looked like a fast blur rather than a perceptible motion. The floor
  keeps short-distance triggers (e.g. re-approaching after scrolling back up partway) feeling snappy; the
  cap keeps long-distance triggers from feeling sluggish.
- Everything else (down-only trigger direction, manual `requestAnimationFrame`-driven animation rather than
  browser-delegated smooth-scroll, `preventDefault()` capture on `wheel`/`touchmove`, desktop/tablet-only
  scope, `prefers-reduced-motion` gating) is unchanged from the prior round.

Verified: `npm run check`/`test:unit`/`build` pass. Confirmed the new threshold (`>30`) and distance-scaled
duration formula (`Math.min(1200, 400 + distance*0.3)`) both compiled correctly into the homepage script
across all 3 languages. Started a fresh preview server, confirmed the same logic in the live-served page,
ran the full route smoke test (all pass), then stopped both the preview process and its `workerd` child
cleanly — three leftover `workerd` processes from earlier verification rounds were found still running
despite last task's cleanup note, confirming that pattern needs to be applied every single time, not just
once it's noticed.

**Honesty note**: the trigger-timing change and duration formula are both straightforward, low-risk logic
changes verified by direct inspection of the compiled output — but the actual *feel* (is 30px the right
threshold, does the distance-scaled duration read as smooth rather than either too fast or too slow at the
now-likely-longer typical distance) is exactly the kind of thing that needs the user's own live judgment,
same limitation as every scroll-timing change on this page so far.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 21: "Un vino che esalta i sensi" closing section — solid black background, section expanded to fill the gap before the footer

User asked to change the background of the homepage's final content section (the "Un vino che esalta i
sensi..." text/button block, immediately before the footer) from its original photo
(`rigoni-barique-riflessi-c.jpg`) to a plain black background, and separately to "fill the gap that sits
between that group and the end of the page with the group itself" — inviting alternatives if the request
wasn't clear. Presented two options via `AskUserQuestion`: (a) expand the section itself to fill the
remaining space and center the text/button group within it, or (b) leave the section's own height as-is and
instead add a plain black spacer between it and the footer. **User chose (a).**

Investigation before implementing:
- Confirmed the section is the last content block before `</article>` (immediately followed by the
  footer) in all 3 language `home.html` files.
- Confirmed the gap's cause: the section's `.grids-area.bottone-centro` text/button content only occupies
  rows 1–3 of the section's own 6-row grid definition (`--_gs-rows:6`); the Grids plugin auto-sizes each row
  to its content, so the unused rows 4–6 collapse to zero height — the section (and its background) renders
  far shorter than its 6-row definition implies, leaving visible empty space before the footer.
- Checked text color before assuming a black background would need any text-color follow-up fix: the
  theme's `:root .has-background-color { color: #f5efe0 }` rule already gives this block's text a light
  cream color (chosen for the original dark photo), so no text-color change was needed.

Implementation:
- `src/content/main/home.html`, `en/home.html`, `de/home.html`: replaced the section's background image
  with solid black across all 3 responsive custom properties (`--_gs-bg-desktop`, `--_gs-bg-tablet`,
  `--_gs-bg-mobile`), from `transparent url(.../rigoni-barique-riflessi-c.jpg) no-repeat 50% 50% / cover` to
  `#000 none`, in all 3 language files (verified via grep: exactly 3 replacements per file, 0 remaining
  references to the old image filename in any of them).
- Added a `home-closing-section` class to the section's outer `.grids-section` div in all 3 files, as a
  scoping hook for the new CSS rule below (this section has no other class distinguishing it from any other
  `.grids-section` on the page).
- `public/styles/site.css`: added
  ```css
  .home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i {
    min-height: 70vh;
    align-content: center;
  }
  ```
  `min-height` targets `.grids-s-w_i` (the inner wrapper whose own `::before` pseudo-element paints the
  section's actual visible background — the same mechanism documented in Task 20's bug-fix notes above) so
  the background itself grows to fill the gap, not just an outer element the background doesn't follow.
  `align-content: center` then centers the 3-row content block as a group within that extra height, rather
  than leaving it pinned to the top with visible black space below it. `70vh` was chosen as a large,
  clearly-intentional-looking height without claiming the full viewport, since the footer still needs its
  own space below it — this is a first pass; exact proportions are exactly the kind of visual-feel judgment
  call flagged in `CLAUDE.md`'s verification-rigor rules as needing the user's own live look, not something
  to over-tune blind. Applied at all screen sizes (not desktop-gated) since this is a plain layout rule with
  no touch/scroll-interaction complexity, unlike Task 17/18's JS.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, the 1 pre-existing unrelated hint in `index.astro`),
`npm run test:unit` (42/42 pass), `npm run build` (succeeds). Confirmed in the compiled `dist/client/`
output: `home-closing-section` class and `--_gs-bg-desktop:#000 none` both present in `it/index.html`,
`en/index.html`, and `de/index.html`; the new `.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i` rule present in the
compiled `dist/client/styles/site.css`. Started a fresh preview server, confirmed the class and background
live in the served `/it/` page, ran the full route smoke test (all routes pass), then killed both the
`astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: same limitation as every visual change on this project — Playwright/Chromium can't launch
in this sandbox, so the actual look (does 70vh feel right, does the centered group read as premium rather
than sparse) needs the user's own live confirmation, not just this static/compiled-output verification.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 21 follow-up: the black background was confirmed live, but the 70vh guess still left a gap before the footer

User confirmed the black background itself renders correctly, but reported a real bug: below the
closing section there's still a visible gap before the footer, and through it they could see "the carousel
of pictures that make up the background of the site" — i.e. the homepage's fixed-position hero
(`.hero-slider`, `position: fixed` on desktop/tablet, Task 17/18) bleeding through, still fully visible
because nothing opaque covers that stretch of viewport.

Root cause, confirmed by reading `site.css`'s existing Task 17 comment (not re-derived from scratch — this
exact mechanism was already documented): `.home-content` (which wraps the whole homepage body below the
hero) gets `position: relative; z-index: 500` specifically so its own content paints above the fixed hero;
`#site-footer` gets the same treatment separately since it's a sibling of `.home-content`, not a descendant.
Between the bottom of `.home-content`'s actual rendered box and the top of `#site-footer`'s box, neither
element's stacking context covers the viewport — so any real, uncovered gap there exposes the still-fixed
hero underneath. The first fix attempt (`min-height: 70vh` on `.grids-s-w_i`) grew the section, but 70vh was
a guessed round number, not a measurement of the actual remaining space, so it undershot on the user's
screen and the gap (and the bleed-through) persisted, just smaller than before.

This is exactly the failure mode `CLAUDE.md`'s verification-rigor rules warn about ("a plausible-sounding
round number" instead of deriving the value from the real page). Rather than guess a second, bigger number
— which would still be fragile to viewport size, font-loading reflow, or future content changes — switched
to a measured, correct-by-construction fix, matching the precedent already set by this same file's
`--header-height` custom property (read from the header's real rendered height, not assumed).

Implementation — `public/styles/site.css`: removed the flat `min-height: 70vh` from
`.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i`, keeping only `align-content: center` (still correct — it centers
whatever content within whatever height the section ends up at, regardless of how that height is set).

`src/components/Hero.astro`: added `initClosingSectionFill()`, a new IIFE following the same conventions as
the file's existing `initHeroFullViewport()`/`initPhilosophySectionBrake()` (this file is the established
home for homepage-only scroll/layout JS). It measures the real gap between `.home-closing-section
.grids-s-w_i` and `#site-footer` via `getBoundingClientRect()`, resets `min-height` to `0px` first so a
shrink is measured correctly (not stuck at a stale larger value), then sets `min-height` to the section's
natural height plus the measured gap — so the section always grows to exactly touch the footer, on any
viewport, regardless of how tall the rest of the page is. Re-runs on `resize`, on `load` (catches any
late-settling layout), and via a `ResizeObserver` on `<body>` (catches content-height changes after the
initial run, e.g. late web-font swaps). Reasoned through the ResizeObserver feedback-loop risk (the fill
script's own DOM write changes body's size, which is exactly what it's observing): confirmed it converges
rather than loops, because reapplying the same computed height on the second pass doesn't produce a further
size change, so the observer has nothing new to fire on.

Not desktop/tablet-scoped like Task 17/18's other effects: the bleed-through problem is specifically caused
by the fixed hero (desktop/tablet-only), but the fill script itself is harmless on mobile too (any measured
gap there is filled the same way; if there's no gap, `minHeight` stays `0px`), so no media-query gating was
added — simpler than replicating the matchMedia scoping for a script with no meaningful mobile downside.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. The new script is inlined and minified into the compiled homepage
HTML (Astro bundles Hero.astro's `<script>` as an inline module) — confirmed via grep for the literal
selector strings `.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i`, `#site-footer`, and `ResizeObserver`, all present in
`dist/client/it/index.html`, `en/index.html`, and `de/index.html` (the function name itself,
`initClosingSectionFill`, is minified away, so string literals were the correct verification target, not
the function name). Confirmed the old static `min-height: 70vh` is no longer present anywhere in the
compiled `site.css`. Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass), then killed both the
`astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: same standing limitation — this is verified as logically sound and structurally present in
the compiled output, but whether it actually closes the gap pixel-for-pixel still needs the user's own live
check, since Playwright can't run here. Unlike the previous attempt though, this isn't a guessed number
that could simply be wrong for their specific viewport — if there's still a gap after this, the bug would
have to be in the *measurement* itself (e.g. an element not found, a wrong assumption about which box is
the true visible bottom edge), a meaningfully different and narrower class of bug than "the guessed number
was too small."

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 21 second follow-up: the measured-fill approach also failed live — switched to growing the footer instead of the section

User confirmed rebuild/restart wasn't the issue (`npm run build` is run after every submission), ruling out
the staleness theory — the JS-measured `min-height` fill genuinely did not close the gap live, a second
consecutive live failure of the same underlying strategy ("grow `.grids-s-w_i` from the inside"). Per
`CLAUDE.md`'s circuit-breaker rule, did not attempt a third incremental patch to the same architecture —
investigated for a structurally different cause instead of adjusting numbers/timing again.

User's own hypothesis was "a margin on the footer" — checked directly: `geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css`
has `#site-footer{...margin:0!important;padding:6rem 0!important;...}`, so footer margin is explicitly
zeroed with `!important`; ruled out as the direct cause (communicated to the user with the exact rule as
evidence, not just an assertion).

Investigated further and found a concrete, different candidate: `#site-content` (the shared `<main>`
wrapper around the entire page body, used by *every* page, not homepage-specific) has `overflow: hidden`
set on it in two theme stylesheets (`twentytwenty-style.min.css` and `parent-style.min.css`). Growing
something deep inside an `overflow: hidden` ancestor via `min-height` (as both prior attempts did, on
`.grids-s-w_i`, several DOM levels inside `#site-content`) doesn't reliably repaint the way ordinary block
growth does, even when the style value itself is being set correctly on the target element — a different
failure class than "the number was too small", and one that wouldn't show up from statically reading CSS
specificity/cascade rules (both prior attempts' CSS was, and remains, textually correct).

Rather than test this theory by changing `overflow` on `#site-content` directly — a shared, sitewide
element outside this task's scope, which per `CLAUDE.md` needs explicit approval given the blast radius,
and which earlier session history already flagged as serving a real purpose (making it the page's scroll
container for a since-abandoned scroll-snap attempt, per Task 18's history) — chose a structurally different
fix that sidesteps the question entirely: `#site-footer` is a *sibling* of `#site-content`, not a
descendant, so it isn't subject to that clipping regardless of whether the theory is exactly right.

Implementation:
- `src/components/Hero.astro`: `initClosingSectionFill()` still measures the same real gap (between
  `.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i`'s bottom edge and `#site-footer`'s top edge, via
  `getBoundingClientRect()`), but instead of setting `min-height` on the section, it sets a
  `--closing-gap-fill` custom property on `#site-footer` itself. Same reset-then-remeasure convergence
  behavior and `resize`/`load`/`ResizeObserver` re-trigger conditions as before.
- `public/styles/site.css`: replaced the `.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i { align-content: center }`
  rule (now vestigial — nothing sets a min-height on that element anymore) with
  `body.home #site-footer::before { position: absolute; left:0; right:0; bottom:100%; height:
  var(--closing-gap-fill, 0px); background: #000; }` — `bottom: 100%` anchors the pseudo-element's own
  bottom edge to the top edge of `#site-footer` (its containing block, since footer already has `position:
  relative` — Task 17/18), so it extends upward by exactly `height`, painting solid black directly above the
  footer. Scoped to the same `@media (min-width: 769px)` block as that existing `position: relative` rule,
  since the pseudo-element's `position: absolute` needs it to anchor correctly, and mobile has no
  fixed-hero bleed-through to cover in the first place (the hero isn't `position: fixed` there either).
  Being a child of `#site-footer`, the pseudo-element automatically participates in footer's own existing
  `z-index: 500` stacking context — no separate z-index needed.
- Known, disclosed tradeoff: this no longer grows the "Un vino..." section's own box, so the text/button
  group is not literally centered within the full combined black area anymore (it sits at the top of its
  own short box; the rest of the black extends from the footer's side, visually seamless but not the same
  DOM element). Flagged this to the user rather than silently changing the approved "expand and center"
  design's centering behavior.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed `--closing-gap-fill` present in the compiled homepage HTML
for all 3 languages (`dist/client/{it,en,de}/index.html`) and the new `#site-footer::before` rule with
`var(--closing-gap-fill, 0px)` present in the compiled `site.css`. The one remaining match for
`.home-closing-section .grids-s-w_i` in the compiled JS is the measurement target (`querySelector`), not
leftover growth logic — confirmed the old min-height-setting code path is gone. Fresh preview server, full
route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes afterward.

**Honesty note**: same standing limitation — logically sound and structurally verified in the compiled
output, but the actual live result still needs the user's own check. This is a genuinely different
mechanism from both prior attempts (extends the footer instead of growing the section), chosen specifically
because it doesn't depend on the `overflow: hidden` theory being exactly correct — but I still can't rule
out an entirely different cause without browser access. Also flagged the centering tradeoff above rather
than let it surface as a surprise if the user notices the text isn't dead-center in the full area.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

image directly (not guessed from the filename) to confirm before proceeding: it's a production-line photo of
a sparkling wine bottle with visible foam overflowing at the neck — matches "a bottle overflowing" exactly.

it's the white "V" logo watermark (the same decorative mark removed from the hero in Task 19), not a bottle,

home-closing-section__image` immediately before the existing `.grids-area bottone-centro` (text) block,
inside the same `home-closing-section`. Positioned at `--_ga-column:1/7` (left half of the 12-column
section, columns 1-6) with the same `--_ga-row:1/4` as the text block (columns 7-12), so they sit side by
side at desktop/tablet widths. Contains a single `<img>` (no `<figure>` wrapper, matching this project's own

codebase — not a Task 21 accessibility regression, an existing sitewide pattern).

`display:grid` to `display:flex;flex-direction:column` at `max-width:768px` (a rule already in the codebase,
not added by this task) — at that breakpoint the `--_ga-column`/`--_ga-row` grid placement becomes irrelevant
and the two `.grids-area` children simply stack in DOM order instead, so the newly-added image (inserted
*before* the text in markup) automatically appears above the text on mobile, a sensible degradation with no
extra CSS required. Confirmed this by reading the existing vendor rule rather than assuming it would "just
work" the way Task 20's/21's other custom sections happen to.

`public/styles/site.css`: added `.home-closing-section__image img { width:100%; height:100%; object-fit:
cover; display:block; }` — `.grids-area` is `display:flex;flex-direction:column` by default but doesn't
itself size a bare `<img>` child to fill it, so this was needed for the image to actually fill its half of
the section rather than rendering at native/intrinsic size. Chose `cover` (not Task 20's `contain`)
deliberately: Task 20 needed `contain` specifically because its 6 product-bottle photos had inconsistent
headroom that `cover` cropped inconsistently across the set; this is a single, one-off editorial/production
photo already used full-bleed (i.e. already cropped via `cover`) on `cantina.html`, so `cover` matches how
the same asset is already trusted to look elsewhere on the site, not a fresh judgment call.

(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed `home-closing-section__image` and the image's compiled

## Task 21 third addition: shrank the cantina photo 20% and gave it gutter space, reusing this page's existing spacing conventions

User confirmed the gap-fill (previous entry) is working live, then asked, in the same message, for a follow-up
on the newly-added photo: shrink it 20% and give it space from the page's own borders and from the text,
"coherent with border conventions on this page" — with the same standing invitation to ask for clarification
if not understood. Treated as two literally separate, both-actionable requests rather than one blended ask,
since that's the plainest reading and avoids inventing a single compound mechanism to serve both at once:

1. **20% smaller** — the picture's own rendered size, independent of any spacing around it.
2. **Gutter space from the page border and from the text** — inset spacing, explicitly asked to match this
   page's own existing conventions rather than a freely-chosen new value.

For (2), looked for what "this page's border conventions" actually are rather than guessing: the adjacent
text block (`.bottone-centro`, same section) already has `--_ga-p-desktop:0 150px 0 0` / `--_ga-p-tablet:
0 150px 0 0` — 150px padding, already established as this section's own real desktop/tablet inset-from-page-
edge convention (it's what keeps the text off the page's right border). The section's own outer wrapper
(`home-closing-section`'s `--_gs-p-mobile:25px 25px 25px 25px`) is the corresponding mobile-scale
convention. Reused both numbers directly instead of inventing new ones: the image's own
`--_ga-p-desktop`/`--_ga-p-tablet` changed from `0 0 0 0` to `0 150px 0 150px` (150px from the page's left
edge, matching the text's own right-edge inset; 150px from the text, reusing the same number for the
inter-element gap rather than adding a third, invented magnitude), and `--_ga-p-mobile` changed from

## Task 21 fourth addition: uncropped the photo and matched font size, using "Dalla campagna al bicchiere" as the reference layout

User reported the previous round's `object-fit:cover` cropped the photo — didn't want cropping, wanted a
"proportionate size with the rest of this group", and pointed at the homepage's first section, "Dalla
campagna al bicchiere" (`.home-philosophy-section`), as the reference to match, plus asked to reduce the
closing section's text size if it differs from that reference. Explicitly flagged as a new design
instruction, not a bug-fix request for the earlier gap-fill work. Also asked, if misunderstood, for
alternative descriptions rather than an overcomplicated attempt — aiming for the cleanest possible code.

Read the reference section's actual markup (`home.html` lines 16-46, not assumed) rather than guessing what
"proportionate" meant:
- Its image is a plain `<figure class="wp-block-image size-large"><img ...></figure>` with **no custom
  sizing CSS at all** — it relies entirely on the sitewide `.wp-block-image img{height:auto;max-width:100%}`
  rule (`wp-global.css`, present since Phase 1, not something this task added) to scale the image down
  proportionally to fit its column width, never cropping, since `object-fit` is never involved.
- Its paragraphs use `class="has-small-font-size wp-block-paragraph"` — the closing section's own paragraph
  did *not* have that class, so it was rendering at the larger default paragraph size while the reference's
  was smaller. Confirmed this was the actual difference (not the heading — both sections' `<h2>` already
  share the same unstyled default size, no divergent CSS found for either one).

Given the reference achieves both non-cropping *and* proportional sizing with zero custom CSS, matched it
exactly rather than inventing a new sizing scheme:
- `src/content/main/home.html`, `en/home.html`, `de/home.html`: wrapped the closing section's `<img>` in
  `<figure class="wp-block-image size-large">...</figure>`, matching the reference's markup precisely (this
  alone is what makes the sitewide `height:auto;max-width:100%` rule apply — it targets `.wp-block-image
  img`, which the previous bare `<img>` never matched). Added `has-small-font-size` to the closing section's
  `<p>` in all 3 languages, matching the reference's paragraph class exactly.
- `public/styles/site.css`: **deleted** the `.home-closing-section__image img { width:80%; height:80%;
  object-fit:cover; display:block; margin:auto; }` rule from the previous round entirely — it's now fully
  redundant (the figure-wrapper approach above does the same non-cropped, proportional job for free) and
  was also the direct cause of the cropping being fixed here, so keeping any variant of it around would
  contradict "as clean as possible." Net effect: less custom CSS in this section than before this change,
  not more.
- The gutter padding (150px desktop/tablet, 25px mobile) added in the previous round on
  `.home-closing-section__image` itself was left untouched — this request was specifically about the
  image's own cropping/sizing and the text's font size, not the gutter spacing, and nothing the user said
  contradicted it.

## Task 21 fifth addition: grew the photo ~30% by shrinking its gutter padding

no custom width/height), the image already renders at the full width of its own grid area's *content* box —
i.e. it's already maxed out relative to the space actually available to it. The only way to make it bigger,
without reintroducing the cropping this section explicitly moved away from last round, is to give that
content box more room — which is exactly the "space to grow" the user was pointing at: the 150px/100px-ish
desktop gutter padding added two rounds ago.

reduction, applied proportionally to keep the desktop:mobile gutter ratio roughly consistent with before,
rather than picking an unrelated mobile number). Estimated the reduction from the section's known 12-column
layout (image spans half the section, roughly 620px on a ~1240px content width) — reducing 300px of total
horizontal padding to 200px frees roughly 100px of width, which computes to roughly a 30% growth of the
~320px the image previously had to work with. Flagged as an estimate, not a browser-measured exact value,

from `0 150px 0 150px` to `0 100px 0 100px`, and `--_ga-p-mobile` from `0 25px 0 25px` to `0 17px 0 17px`,
identically in all 3 languages. No CSS changes needed — the sizing mechanism (the sitewide
`.wp-block-image img` rule from the previous round) automatically fills whatever content-box width the
padding now leaves available; only the padding input changed.

## Task 21 sixth addition: reintroduced a slight side crop, made the photo bigger, and diagnosed a real (not cosmetic) off-center bug

User asked for three things in one message: (a) slightly crop the photo on the sides, (b) make it bigger,
and (c) a direct answer — is it actually off-center, or does it just look that way? Explicitly a new
instruction, and explicitly asked to confirm understanding with alternatives if unclear; understood clearly
enough to proceed without asking.

Investigated (c) first, since it's a factual question answerable from the CSS, not a matter of taste:
`.grids-area` (`home-closing-section__image`) is `display:flex;flex-direction:column` with the default
`align-items:stretch` — this stretches the `<figure>` (a plain, non-replaced block box) to 100% of the
column's width. But the `<img>` inside it, from the previous round, had **no explicit width** — just the
sitewide `.wp-block-image img{height:auto;max-width:100%}` rule, which only *caps* size, it doesn't force
fill. An `<img>` is inline-level by default with no `text-align:center`/`margin:auto` set anywhere on the
figure to center it, so whenever the image's own intrinsic render size came in under the figure's full
(stretched) width, it sat flush at the figure's start edge — the figure filled the column, the image inside
it didn't, leaving real, asymmetric empty space on one side. Confirmed via the actual cascade (figure's
stretch vs. the image's separate sizing rule), not inferred from a screenshot. **Answer given to the user:
this was a real bug, not just an appearance** — the mismatch between what stretches (the figure) and what
doesn't (the image inside it).

The fix for (c) also directly serves (a) and (b): giving the `<img>` itself `width:100%` makes it always
fill its figure (removing the leftover space that caused the visual imbalance, and independently making it
render larger than its previous, sometimes-smaller intrinsic size — the "bigger" ask). Combined with a fixed
`aspect-ratio:4/3` + `object-fit:cover`, since 4/3 is narrower than the source photo's native ~3:2 ratio,
`cover` scales the image to fill that taller-relative-to-width box, cropping a modest amount off the left/
right sides while keeping the full bottle height in frame — the "slight side crop" ask. `4/3` was chosen as
a common, nameable ratio close to a mild crop of ~3:2, not an arbitrary decimal, in the same spirit as
reusing existing numbers in earlier rounds of this task.

`public/styles/site.css`: replaced the previous round's comment-only block (documenting the deliberately
CSS-free approach) with `.home-closing-section__image img { display:block; width:100%; aspect-ratio:4/3;
object-fit:cover; }`. No markup changes needed in any of the 3 `home.html` files this round — the `<figure
class="wp-block-image size-large">` wrapper from two rounds ago is unchanged and still correct; only the
`<img>`'s own sizing behavior needed to change.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed the new `width:100%;aspect-ratio:4/3;object-fit:cover`
rule present in the compiled `site.css`. Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass),
killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: the *diagnosis* (figure stretches, image didn't) is a structural, verifiable fact from the
CSS cascade, not a guess — high confidence there. Whether the resulting crop amount (4:3) reads as "slight"
rather than too aggressive or still too subtle is, as always, the user's own call to make live.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

# Task 22: footer logo swap + address column replaced with a "go to contacts" link

New task, distinct from Task 21 (the closing "Un vino..." section) — this one is the site-wide footer
(`src/content/chrome/footer.html`, `en/footer.html`, `de/footer.html`), shared by every page, not just the
homepage. Two asks: (1) swap the footer's own company logo for the same one used in the header/nav (top
left), and (2) replace the address block in the footer's icon row with a centered "go to contacts" link,
styled like the site's other section titles (e.g. "Dalla campagna al bicchiere"), linking to each language's
real contact page.

Investigation:
- Read `footer.html` directly: the footer logo (`.footer-row-logo img`) used
  `/wp-content/uploads/2020/12/rigoni-vittorino.png`; the header's logo (`header.html`,
  `.site-logo img`) uses `/wp-content/uploads/2021/01/rigoni-vittorino.png` — a **different file**, not
  just a different display size. Viewed both directly (not assumed from filenames): the 2020/12 file is an
  older sans-serif single-line wordmark with an Italian-flag stripe underneath; the 2021/01 file is the
  current two-line serif wordmark used everywhere else on the site today. Confirmed intrinsic dimensions via
  PIL: 2020/12 file is 200×94 (matches its previous display size exactly, no scaling); 2021/01 file is
  1178×489 (~2.409:1, wider proportionally than the old file's ~2.128:1) — so swapping the `src` alone
  without adjusting `width`/`height` would have visibly distorted it. Set `width="226" height="94"` (94px
  height matches the previous footer visual height; 226 is 94×2.409 rounded, preserving the new file's real
  aspect ratio) — moot in practice since `.footer-row-logo img{max-width:100%;height:auto}`
  (`geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css`) actually governs the rendered size, but set accurately anyway to
  avoid a layout-shift flash before the image loads. Also set `alt="Rigoni Vittorino"`, matching the
  header's own alt text (previously empty).
- Found the real, currently-applied h2 styling by reading the cascade, not assuming: a bare, unscoped
  `h2{font-size:36px;line-height:4.8rem}` in `geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css` loads *after* the parent
  twentytwenty theme's own responsive/media-query h2 sizing, and wins at equal specificity by load order —
  so every h2 on the site, including "Dalla campagna al bicchiere" and "Un vino che esalta i sensi..."
  (neither has its own font-size override), actually renders at a flat 36px/Cormorant-serif/400-weight
  regardless of viewport. This means reusing the literal `<h2 class="wp-block-heading">` element/class for
  the new link is a byte-for-byte guarantee of matching that styling — no new CSS needed, and no risk of the
  match drifting if the theme's responsive rules ever change. Confirmed `footer#site-footer a{color:
  var(--footer-font-color-link)}` already gives footer links an appropriate color for the dark footer
  background (more specific than the sitewide `body a{color:var(--link-color)}`), so no extra color styling
  was needed either.
- Confirmed `.footer-icon-column{text-align:center;display:flex;flex-direction:column;align-items:center}`
  already centers its own content — no extra centering class needed on the new h2.
- Found each language's real contact-page URL from `src/data/locale-equivalents.json`: `/it/contatti/`,
  `/en/contatti/` (the English site keeps the Italian word "contatti" in its URL — an existing, real route,
  not a typo introduced here), `/de/contacts/`.

Implementation — all 3 footer files (`footer.html`, `en/footer.html`, `de/footer.html`):
- `.footer-row-logo img`: `src` changed to the 2021/01 file, `width`/`height` updated to `226`/`94`, `alt`
  set to `"Rigoni Vittorino"`.
- The first `.footer-icon-column` (previously: address icon image + street address + Google Maps link)
  replaced entirely with `<h2 class="wp-block-heading"><a href="...">...</a></h2>`: `Vai ai contatti` →
  `/it/contatti/` (IT), `Go to contacts` → `/en/contatti/` (EN), `Zu den Kontakten` → `/de/contacts/` (DE).
  The German phrase was translated properly rather than copying the site's own pre-existing "Contacts"
  (English, untranslated) nav-menu quirk on the German site — that quirk is existing, out-of-scope copy this
  task doesn't touch; new copy this task is introducing doesn't need to repeat it. Phone and email columns
  are untouched.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Since the footer is shared chrome (not homepage-only), confirmed the
new logo path and contact link compiled correctly not just on each language's homepage but also on a
non-homepage page (`dist/client/it/chi-siamo/index.html`), confirming the change applies sitewide as
intended rather than accidentally being homepage-scoped. Fresh preview server: confirmed all three real
target URLs (`/it/contatti/`, `/en/contatti/`, `/de/contacts/`) return `200` directly (not just present in
the smoke test's route list). Full route smoke test (all routes pass). Killed both `astro preview` and
`workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: this is a sitewide, shared-chrome change (touches every page in every language) — flagged
per `CLAUDE.md`'s "changes outside the active task's narrow scope" guidance, though in this case the change
*is* the task (footer content, by definition site-wide). Nothing else in the footer or any other page was
touched. Visual confirmation (does the new logo look right at its new size, does the centered link read
correctly) still needs the user's own live check, same standing limitation as every visual change this
session.

## Task 22 follow-up: matched the nav bar's hover stripe on the new "go to contacts" link

User described a visual detail on the top nav bar — hovering a nav item shows a light brown stripe across
its bottom — and asked to confirm recognition of it before applying the same treatment to the new footer
link. Confirmed by re-reading the actual rule (not relying on memory): `geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css`
has `ul.primary-menu>li:hover{background:linear-gradient(0deg,#e2d1b4 45%,rgba(255,0,255,0) 30%)}` — a
bottom-anchored gradient (`0deg` points the gradient line from bottom to top; the tan color stop at 45%
with a transparent stop at a smaller 30% gets clamped to the same position by the CSS spec's monotonic-stop
rule, producing a fairly sharp-edged tan band across roughly the bottom 45% of the item's box) — plus
`ul.primary-menu>li{padding:5px}` giving that box some breathing room around the text.

Deliberately used the *hover* color (`#e2d1b4`), not the visually similar `#DCC59F` the same stylesheet uses
for `ul.primary-menu>li.current-menu-item` (the nav's *active/current-page* indicator, a different state
with a different, if closely related, shade) — the user specifically described hovering, not an active-page
indicator, so matching the wrong one would have been a subtly incorrect copy despite looking almost right.

`public/styles/site.css`: added `.footer-contact-link { display:inline-block; padding:5px; }` and
`.footer-contact-link:hover { background: linear-gradient(0deg, #e2d1b4 45%, rgba(255,0,255,0) 30%); }` —
reusing the nav's exact gradient formula and padding value rather than approximating them, so this reads as
the same interaction pattern, not a similar-looking new one. `display:inline-block` makes the padding/
background render as a clean box around the short link text, the way it already does around a nav `<li>`.

`src/content/chrome/footer.html`, `en/footer.html`, `de/footer.html`: added `class="footer-contact-link"` to
the "go to contacts" anchor added in the previous round, in all 3 languages. No other markup changes.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed `footer-contact-link` present in the compiled homepage
HTML for all 3 languages and both new CSS rules present in the compiled `site.css`. Fresh preview server,
full route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: the CSS values themselves are copied verbatim from the nav's own rule, so there's no
approximation risk in the *values* — but whether the hover box's exact width/height (governed by the
`display:inline-block` + `padding:5px` box around this specific short phrase) visually reads the same as a
nav item's own box is, as always, the user's own call to make live.

a Font Awesome WhatsApp brand mark → phone (WhatsApp is fundamentally a phone-number-based messaging app,
the only sensible mapping), and a "reply" arrow glyph → email (the conventional icon for a reply/email
action). Proceeded without asking given only one mapping made sense with two files and two slots, unlike

## Task 22 follow-up: fixed real vertical-centering bug on the contact link, reordered columns

User reported the contact link column looked off-center, and asked for it moved to the second column
(phone, contact link, email order) — treated the centering report as a real bug to diagnose, not just an
appearance question, per this project's own established pattern for exactly this phrasing.

Diagnosed by reading the actual CSS rather than guessing: `.footer-icon-row{display:flex;justify-content:
center}` has no `align-items` set, so its default value (`stretch`) makes every `.footer-icon-column` child
the same height as the tallest one — the phone/email columns, each holding an icon (52px) plus a text line.
`.footer-icon-column{...display:flex;flex-direction:column;align-items:center}` only centers its own content
*horizontally* (that's what `align-items:center` does on its own cross axis); with no `justify-content` set,
a stretched-taller column's shorter content sits at the top by default, not centered in the extra height.
The contact link (a single short line) is shorter than the phone/email columns' icon+text stacks, so once
the row stretched it to match their height, it visibly sat higher than its neighbors — a real, structural
cause, not just a rendering quirk.

`public/styles/site.css`: added `.footer-contact-column { justify-content: center; }` — scoped to a *second*
class added only to this specific column (`footer-icon-column footer-contact-column`), not a change to the
shared `.footer-icon-column` rule itself, since that class is vendor CSS also used for the phone/email
columns (and potentially elsewhere) where this behavior was never a problem.

`src/content/chrome/footer.html`, `en/footer.html`, `de/footer.html`: reordered `.footer-icon-row`'s three
children to phone, contact link, email (previously phone, email, contact link), and added the
`footer-contact-column` class to the contact link's wrapper in all 3 languages.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint — this run took slightly
longer than the 120s foreground timeout and was moved to background automatically; confirmed its completed
output directly, same clean result as every other round), `npm run test:unit` (42/42 pass), `npm run build`
succeeds. Confirmed via direct inspection of the compiled HTML that the column order is phone → contact link
→ email, and the `footer-contact-column` class + its `justify-content:center` rule are both present, for
all 3 languages. Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview`
and `workerd serve` processes.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 22 second follow-up: found the real remaining causes of the vertical shift and the row-vs-logo misalignment

User reported two things in one message: the contact link was still visibly shifted down despite the
previous round's fix, and separately, the whole icon row didn't line up under the footer logo — asked
explicitly to confirm understanding or offer alternatives if the fix looked unreasonably complex. Understood
both clearly and found concrete, different root causes for each by reading further into the cascade, rather
than guessing a second value for the first one or assuming a general "not centered" report already covered
the second.

**Vertical shift, cause #2**: `twentytwenty/parent-style.min.css` has `h1,.heading-size-1,h2,.heading-size-2,
h3,.heading-size-3{margin:6rem auto 3rem}` — every h1/h2/h3 gets an asymmetric 96px-top/48px-bottom default
margin. The later, more specific `body h2{...margin-left:0;margin-right:0}` rule (geppa/twentytwenty-
style.min.css) only ever zeroes *left/right* margin, never top/bottom — confirmed by re-reading that exact
rule, not assumed from memory. The contact link is the only one of the three footer columns built from an
`<h2>` (phone/email are plain `<div>`s, never subject to this rule at all), so it alone carried this leftover
96px push downward, fighting the previous round's `justify-content:center` fix rather than being fixed by
it.

**Row-vs-logo misalignment, a separate bug**: `.footer-icon-row{display:flex;justify-content:center}`
centers the *combined width* of its 3 children as one block — that only coincides with the row's true
center (and thus the logo's own centered position above it) when the flanking columns are equal width.
"Email: info@rigonivittorino.com" (32 characters) is visibly longer than "Tel. +39 0422.746459" (21
characters), so the block is lopsided and the middle column's own center drifts off by roughly half that
imbalance — this was never fixed by the earlier vertical-centering work, since it's a horizontal, row-level
issue, not a per-column one.

`public/styles/site.css`:
- `.footer-contact-column .wp-block-heading { margin: 0; }` — zeroes the leftover top/bottom margin, scoped
  to just this column's heading so it doesn't touch any other h2 on the site.
- `@media (min-width: 769px) { .footer-icon-row { display: grid; grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr); } }`
  — switches the row to 3 equal-width grid columns, a correct-by-construction fix (the middle column's
  center is then guaranteed to match the row's true center regardless of how long the phone/email text is,
  rather than one that happens to work only for today's specific strings). Scoped to `min-width:769px`,
  matching the vendor's own existing `max-width:768px` mobile override for this same class — reasoned through
  the risk explicitly: an unscoped version would load after, and therefore win over, that mobile-only rule
  (same specificity, later in the cascade), silently forcing 3 equal columns on mobile too and breaking the
  intentional single-column stacking there. No markup changes needed in any of the 3 `footer.html` files
  this round — both fixes are CSS-only.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed both new rules — the margin reset and the
`min-width:769px`-scoped grid override — present in the compiled `site.css`. Fresh preview server, full
route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: both causes are concrete, verifiable facts from the actual cascade (an asymmetric margin
rule, and an unequal-width centering calculation) — not guesses — and the second fix in particular is
structurally guaranteed to keep working regardless of future copy changes to the phone/email text, unlike a
fix that happened to look right for today's specific strings. Still, whether the two fixes together fully
resolve what the user is seeing needs their own live check, same standing limitation as every visual change
this session.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

# Task 23: language switcher restored, site-wide overscroll bounce disabled

New task, site-wide/general scope (not homepage-specific), started with "let's move to general changes of
the landing page" but the language-switcher fix is a site-wide header change and the overscroll change is
explicitly general "quality" polish, not scoped to one page. Two requests in one message, both understood
without needing clarification.

**Language switcher missing**: user asked whether it was hidden behind the nav. Investigated and explained
rather than assumed: it wasn't a stacking/z-index bug — `header#site-header .pre-header-inner { display:
none !important; }` in `public/styles/site.css` was a deliberate, explicit decision from an earlier task
(the header used to shrink on scroll; that task locked it permanently into its compact "scrolled"
appearance, hiding the language switcher/social-icons bar as part of that). Flagged this clearly as
reopening frozen work per the user's own explicit new request, not a silent reversal. Changed `display:
none !important` to `display: flex !important` (`flex` is `.pre-header-inner`'s own natural value from the
vendor CSS, `geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css`) — kept the same "nothing about the header changes visually
on scroll" principle intact by forcing it visible *unconditionally*, not just at the top: the vendor CSS also
has a separate `header#site-header.sticky .pre-header-inner{display:none}` scroll-triggered hide rule (no
`!important` of its own, confirmed by re-reading it directly), which this `!important` override beats
regardless of `.sticky`'s higher selector specificity — otherwise the switcher would reappear at the top but
vanish again on scroll, reintroducing a partial version of the exact resize-on-scroll behavior the earlier
task removed.

**Overscroll bounce**: read as a request to disable the rubber-band/elastic overscroll effect (scrolling
past the very top briefly reveals blank space before springing back — common on trackpads/Safari and
mobile), a standard "app-like polish" technique. Added `html { overscroll-behavior-y: none; }`, applied to
`html` specifically because this project's own extensive scroll-jacking code (`Hero.astro`, Task 17/18)
already relies on `window.scrollY`/`window.scrollTo` and is confirmed working live — which is only possible
if `html`/`window` is genuinely the page's real scrolling root, not some nested wrapper (a fact already
established by this project's own prior, live-verified behavior, not re-derived from scratch here).

`public/styles/site.css`: both changes, as described above.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint — the first attempt this
round hit the 120s foreground timeout and moved to background, then that background run's own completion
notification came back as "stopped" with no output on a later check, likely from a session boundary; re-ran
it fresh directly afterward and got a clean result, so no result was ever assumed without actually seeing
one), `npm run test:unit` (42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed `display:flex !important` and
`overscroll-behavior-y:none` both present in the compiled `site.css` (this file is shared/global, so no
per-language checking was needed — unlike page-content changes, a global stylesheet change applies
identically everywhere by construction). Fresh preview server: confirmed `pre-header-inner` markup renders
on the live served page. Full route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and `workerd
serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: same standing limitation as every visual change — the CSS is correct by the cascade rules
and mirrors the reasoning already established for the adjacent, already-approved logo-size rule, but the
actual live look/feel (does the switcher look right always-visible, does the overscroll change feel right)
needs the user's own check, since Playwright can't run here.

## Task 23 follow-up: language switcher scroll behavior refined; fade transitions added between the homepage's stacked sections

Two requests, both understood without needing clarification.

**Header refinement**: last round made the pre-header bar (language switcher/socials) visible
*unconditionally* — this round's request narrowed that to "only at top," while explicitly keeping the logo
permanently compact regardless of scroll. Confirmed `site.js`'s `stickyHeader()` toggles `.sticky` the
moment `window.scrollY > 0` (immediate, no threshold), so "only at top" maps directly onto "not `.sticky`".
Removed the `header#site-header .pre-header-inner { display: flex !important; }` override entirely, rather
than writing a new rule — with it gone, the vendor's own unmodified pair of rules
(`.pre-header-inner{display:flex}` base + `.sticky .pre-header-inner{display:none}` on scroll) does exactly
what was asked, for free. The logo-size lock (`max-height:50px!important`) is untouched.

**Fade transitions**: user asked for a slight fade between the backgrounds of 4 stacked homepage
groups/areas — "Dalla campagna al bicchiere", "La nostra collezione", "Un vino che esalta i sensi...", and
the footer — i.e. 3 boundaries. Read each section's actual background color from the markup rather than
guessing: philosophy section is `transparent` (revealing the page's own `--background-color:#f8f8f8`, from
`wp-global.css`); "La nostra collezione" (`.section-news`) is `#ece9e4`; the closing section is `#000`
(Task 21); the footer is `#ffffff` (`--footer-background-color`).

Two of the three boundaries are straightforward: added a fixed 100px `::after` gradient overlay at the very
bottom of the *earlier* section in each pair, fading from transparent (revealing that section's own
unchanged background for most of the overlay's height) to the *next* section's real color, so the blend
visually completes right at the boundary line — `.home-philosophy-section::after`
(transparent → `#ece9e4`) and `.section-news::after` (transparent → `#000`). Both anchor on the Grids
plugin's own pre-existing `.grids-section{position:relative}` base rule, so no extra positioning setup was
needed. `pointer-events:none` on both so these purely decorative overlays can never intercept a click.

The third boundary (closing section → footer) needed more thought, since Task 21 already made this specific
boundary dynamic: the visible black area that touches the footer's white isn't `.home-closing-section`'s own
edge — it's the JS-measured `#site-footer::before` gap-fill rectangle (`--closing-gap-fill`), whose height
varies per viewport and can be very small or zero. Fading `.home-closing-section`'s own bottom edge instead
would be wrong on any viewport where a gap exists: both `.home-closing-section` and the gap-fill rectangle
are solid black, so an early fade-to-white there would prematurely turn white, then have to snap back to
black for the gap-fill, then fade to white *again* at the true boundary — a visibly broken double-fade. So
the fade was added to the gap-fill rectangle itself instead, changing its `background: #000` to
`background: linear-gradient(to bottom, #000 0%, #000 70%, #fff 100%)` — deliberately percentage-based, not
a fixed pixel band like the other two, specifically because this element's own height is the dynamic
measured gap: a fixed-pixel fade could be taller than the whole element on a short gap, while a percentage
always fits inside whatever height the element ends up with on any given viewport. Accepted, disclosed
tradeoff: if the real gap on some viewport is 0px (no gap needed at all), this specific fade simply doesn't
render — a graceful "no fade" rather than a broken one, not something requiring a JS change to force a
minimum height purely for a decorative effect.

`public/styles/site.css`: all three changes, as described above. No markup changes in any file this round.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed all three gradient rules present in the compiled `site.css`,
and confirmed the old unconditional `pre-header-inner` override is completely gone from the compiled output
(not just edited-looking-right in source). Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass),
killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: same standing limitation — the header logic is a direct, verifiable read of `site.js`'s
actual scroll threshold, and the gradient placements are grounded in each section's real background color
(not guessed), but the actual visual read (does 100px feel "slight", does the footer fade look right across
different real viewport heights where the gap varies) needs the user's own live check.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 23 correction: removed the fades; replaced the language switcher's hard hide with a real slide transition

User reported the fades didn't look good (removed outright, no replacement requested) and diagnosed the
pre-header bar's disappearance as looking "buggy," asking whether the cause was understood and fixable —
both understood clearly.

**Fades removed**: deleted all three gradient rules added last round — the `#site-footer::before` gradient
reverted to its prior plain `background: #000`, and the two `::after` overlay rules
(`.home-philosophy-section::after`, `.section-news::after`) deleted entirely, including their explanatory
comment block. No markup was ever touched for the fades, so no markup changes were needed to remove them
either.

**Header slide diagnosis**: the vendor's own hide mechanism, `header#site-header.sticky .pre-header-inner{
display: none }`, uses `display` — a property CSS cannot transition/animate under any circumstances, by
spec. Every previous round's behavior for this element (hidden, shown, hidden-then-shown-again) used this
same `display` toggle underneath, so it was *always* an instant, hard cut regardless of which specific rule
was driving it — confirmed as the real, structural cause, not guessed.

Fixed by switching to `max-height` + `opacity`, both genuinely animatable: `header#site-header
.pre-header-inner { overflow:hidden; max-height:60px; opacity:1; transition: max-height .35s ease, opacity
.25s ease; }` with `header#site-header.sticky .pre-header-inner { display:flex!important; max-height:0;
opacity:0; }` — the `display:flex!important` override is necessary specifically to defeat the vendor's own
`display:none` on that state, since an element that's `display:none` is removed from the layout instantly
with nothing left to transition; keeping it `flex` (just visually collapsed via `max-height:0` +
`overflow:hidden`) is what lets the transition actually play in both directions (collapsing on scroll,
expanding back on scroll-to-top). `60px` is a safely-larger-than-content estimate for the collapsed bar
(`font-size:1.3rem` text + small icons), not a browser-measured exact value — flagged as such, since a
max-height transition only needs a value that comfortably contains the content at rest, not an exact one, to
still look like a genuine slide.

`public/styles/site.css`: both changes, as described above.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed all fade-related CSS is completely absent from the
compiled `site.css` (0 matches on a direct grep), confirmed the footer's background reverted to plain
`#000`, and confirmed the new `max-height`/`transition` rules are present. Fresh preview server, full route
smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and `workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: the `display:none`-can't-transition diagnosis is a CSS-spec-level fact, not a guess — high
confidence there. The `60px` collapse height and the `.35s`/`.25s` timing are reasonable defaults, not
browser-measured or user-specified values, so the actual feel of the slide (does it look too fast/slow, is
60px close enough to the real content height to avoid clipping) needs the user's own live check.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

# Task 24: scroll-jacking brake for "La nostra collezione", proximity-triggered

User asked for the same kind of centering scroll animation already built for "Dalla campagna al bicchiere"
(Task 18) to also apply to "La nostra collezione", explicitly asking for a description if needed and
warning against unnecessary complexity. Given Task 18's own history (5 implementation attempts — a CSS
scroll-snap approach confirmed unreliable, a passive post-scroll nudge reported as "groggy", before landing
on an active `requestAnimationFrame`-driven scroll-jacking brake that's been confirmed working live several
times since), reusing that exact proven technique for a second section is clearly the lowest-risk,
least-complex option available — not re-attempting CSS scroll-snap (already ruled out in this codebase) or
inventing a third new mechanism.

Before implementing, confirmed the one open design question via `AskUserQuestion`: how should the brake for
this section actually trigger, since Task 18's own trigger ("as soon as the user starts scrolling down at
all, from page top") makes no sense reused verbatim for a section much further down the page. Presented two
options — proximity-based triggering (matching "when the user slides near", and matching Task 18's own
*original*, pre-refinement trigger design) vs. a passive settle-nudge (explicitly flagged as the technique
already reported unsatisfying for the first section, offered only for completeness, not recommended). **User
chose proximity-based triggering.**

Implementation — `src/components/Hero.astro`, new `initCollectionSectionBrake()` IIFE, added directly after
`initPhilosophySectionBrake()`:
- Targets `.section-news` ("La nostra collezione"'s own class in the scraped markup, confirmed by reading
  `home.html` directly, not assumed from the section's display name).
- Reuses `initPhilosophySectionBrake()`'s exact centering math (`targetScrollY()`, accounting for the
  header's real rendered height), easing function, manual RAF-driven step loop, and
  duration-scales-with-distance formula (400ms floor, 1200ms cap, +0.3ms/px) unchanged — these were already
  tuned/validated for the first section and there's no reason to expect a second section would need
  different easing or duration math.
- **Different, deliberately**: the trigger condition. Instead of a flat "any scroll past 30px from page
  top" threshold, this measures the section's real document-top position (`docTop()`, same helper pattern
  already used in `initHeroFullViewport()`) and computes `triggerY = docTop(section) - innerHeight -
  EARLY_TRIGGER_PX` — the scroll position at which the section is `EARLY_TRIGGER_PX` (200px) away from
  entering the viewport from the bottom. Re-measured on resize (responsive reflow can shift the section's
  real document position). The reset condition (re-arming the brake so scrolling down again later
  re-triggers it) is `currentY < triggerY` — scrolled back above the trigger zone — the natural equivalent
  of Task 18's own "back near page top" reset, adapted for a mid-page target instead of a page-top-anchored
  one.
- `EARLY_TRIGGER_PX = 200` is a reasoned starting point, not a browser-measured exact value — same category
  as Task 18's own `TRIGGER_BUFFER_PX`/`MIN_TRIGGER_SCROLL_PX`, both of which were tuned from live feedback
  rather than derived analytically. Flagged as such rather than presented as precise.

# Task 25: partial fix for a viewport-edge gap on the homepage's stacked sections

User asked, before checking Task 24's brake, whether there's a reason for a gap between the backgrounds of
the homepage's stacked sections ("Dalla campagna al bicchiere" through "Un vino che esalta i sensi...") and
the viewport edge, and whether it can be fixed safely.

Investigated each section's own inline scraped styles directly rather than guessing:
- `.home-philosophy-section`'s inner `.grids-s-w_i` has `--_gs-m-desktop:0 10px 60px 10px` (and the same
  10px left/right at tablet/mobile) — a real, confirmed 10px margin on each side, leaving the page's own
  pale background (`--background-color:#f8f8f8`, `wp-global.css`) visible in a strip at both edges.
- `.section-news` ("La nostra collezione") and `.home-closing-section` ("Un vino che esalta i sensi...")
  both have `--_gs-m-desktop:0 0 0 0` at every breakpoint — no margin or width reduction of their own at
  all. Also checked the ancestor chain for anything that could be constraining them instead: `.post-inner`
  has a `width:90%` rule in the theme CSS, but it's scoped to `body.single-post` specifically — this is
  `body.home`, so it doesn't apply; `#site-content`/`.entry-content` have no unscoped width reduction either
  (a `body.home`-specific rule even explicitly resets non-alignfull `.entry-content` children back to
  `width:auto` on this exact page, overriding a more general theme rule that would otherwise narrow them).
  No structural cause found for these two despite a real investigation, not a quick dismissal.

Given a confirmed cause for one section and none found for the other two, implemented only the confirmed
fix rather than guessing at CSS changes for sections whose own markup shows no reduction: `public/styles/
site.css` — added `.home-philosophy-section .grids-s-w_i { margin-left: 0; margin-right: 0; }`, overriding
only the longhand left/right margin properties (not the `margin` shorthand, which would also need to
redeclare the section's intentional 60px bottom spacing to avoid losing it). Told the user directly, before
implementing, that this only explains/fixes one of the three sections named, and that the other two might be
a related partial effect, an optical illusion (their background colors are close in tone to the page's own
pale background), or something not detectable from the CSS alone without seeing it live.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed the new margin-reset rule present in the compiled
`site.css`. Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and
`workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: this is a *partial* fix, explicitly flagged as such to the user before implementing rather
than silently applying it and hoping it covered everything — the confirmed cause for the first section is
grounded in its own real inline margin value, but whether this alone resolves what the user is seeing on all
three sections, or whether the other two need separate further investigation, is something only their own
live check can settle.

## Task 21/24 correction: reverted the closing-section text width, removed Task 24's brake, added site-wide smooth scroll

Three requests in one message, all understood without needing clarification.

**Text width revert**: user reported the "Un vino che esalta i sensi..." text was now squeezed too much,
and asked for the title specifically not to be squeezed at all. Rather than guess an intermediate column
width, reverted the change from last round entirely — `--_ga-column` back from `9/13` to `7/13` (6 of 12
columns, its state before the "match Dalla campagna al bicchiere's width" request) — in all 3 languages.
This addresses both complaints at once: it's the last width the user hadn't objected to, and the title
naturally has its original room back, not a new, unverified middle-ground guess that might still be "too
squeezed."

**Task 24 brake removed**: user decided the collection-section scroll-jacking brake should be removed
outright, not further tuned. Deleted `initCollectionSectionBrake()` entirely from `Hero.astro` (the whole
IIFE, lines 315-443, including all its comments) — Task 18's own, separate, already-confirmed-working brake
for "Dalla campagna al bicchiere" (`initPhilosophySectionBrake()`) was left completely untouched, since the
user's phrasing ("the animation we were working currently on") referred to the one from the immediately
preceding rounds, not the original, older, separately-accepted one.

**Site-wide smooth scroll**: user asked for smoother sliding through the site, explicitly inviting
alternatives if it sounded complicated. Rather than build any custom JS inertia/momentum-scroll system
(unnecessarily complex, and effectively re-implementing scrolling physics the OS/browser already provides
natively for raw wheel/trackpad/touch input), used the standard, low-complexity CSS technique:
`scroll-behavior: smooth` on `html`, which only affects *programmatic* scrolls (anchor-link jumps, JS
`scrollTo` calls) with an eased transition instead of an instant jump — normal scroll input was already
natively smooth and isn't something worth reaching for more complexity to "improve." Added a
`prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` override back to `scroll-behavior: auto`, matching every other motion
effect on this page.

**Caught and fixed a real risk before it could ship as a bug**: `scroll-behavior: smooth` applies to *any*
scroll triggered via the CSSOM-adjacent 2-argument `window.scrollTo(x, y)` form — which is exactly what
Task 18's still-active brake (`initPhilosophySectionBrake()`'s `step()` function) uses, calling it every
single animation frame with a manually-eased value. Left unchanged, the browser would have tried to layer
its own smooth-scroll animation on top of values that are already being manually eased ~60 times a second —
fighting the existing animation rather than complementing it, a real, concrete regression risk to an
already-confirmed-working feature, not a hypothetical one. Fixed by switching that one call to the explicit
object form with `behavior: "auto"` (`window.scrollTo({top, left: 0, behavior: "auto"})`), which is immune
to the CSS property regardless of its value. Grepped the rest of `Hero.astro` and `site.js` for any other
`scrollTo`/`scrollIntoView` calls that could be similarly affected — none found.

`src/content/main/home.html`, `en/home.html`, `de/home.html`: column-width revert. `src/components/
Hero.astro`: brake removal, scrollTo fix. `public/styles/site.css`: smooth-scroll rule.

Verified: `npm run check` (0 errors, 0 warnings, same 1 pre-existing unrelated hint), `npm run test:unit`
(42/42 pass), `npm run build` succeeds. Confirmed `--_ga-column:7/13` present in compiled output for all 3
languages. Confirmed the brake is *completely* gone — an initial naive check (splitting the raw HTML on
`<script`) falsely suggested a lingering reference, traced to a flawed check (the split miscounted due to
where the class name appears in the page markup relative to script tag boundaries), not a real leftover;
redid the check by properly parsing each `<script>` tag's own inner content, confirming zero actual script
content still references the removed section's selector. Confirmed the `scroll-behavior: smooth`/`auto`
rules present in compiled CSS, and confirmed the fixed `scrollTo({... behavior:"auto"})` call present in the
compiled JS (not just the source — the literal string `behavior:` compiled through minification intact,
since minifiers don't rename object property keys used as string literals in an object literal passed to a
DOM API). Fresh preview server, full route smoke test (all routes pass), killed both `astro preview` and
`workerd serve` processes.

**Honesty note**: the revert and removal are exact, verifiable reversions to prior known states, not new
guesses. The smooth-scroll addition is the standard technique for what was described, with the
Task-18-interaction risk caught and fixed proactively rather than left for a live bug report — but whether
the overall scroll *feel* now matches what the user pictured (given ordinary scroll input itself is
unchanged, only anchor/programmatic scrolls are affected) needs their own live check to confirm this
interpretation was the right one.

Not committed, per this project's standing convention of only committing/pushing when explicitly instructed.

## Task 21 follow-up: image grown 25%, button nudged up, and the "bottone-home" button researched

Three items in one message: two direct implementation requests, one informational question.

**Image +25%**: same lever as the earlier "30% bigger" round — the image is `width:100%` of its own grid
area's padded content box, so growing it means shrinking that padding. Reused the same estimation method
(assumed ~1240px section content width, image column spans half): current 100px×2 padding gives a ~420px
image; 25% bigger is ~525px; solving for the new padding gives ~48px each side. Mobile scaled by the same
proportional factor as before (100→48 is ×0.48; applied to the existing 17px mobile value gives ~8px).
`src/content/main/home.html`, `en/home.html`, `de/home.html`: `--_ga-p-desktop`/`-tablet` 100px→48px,
`--_ga-p-mobile` 17px→8px, identical in all 3 languages.

first rather than writing a blind global override: `.bottone-home` is a *shared* class from the theme CSS
(`geppa/twentytwenty-style.min.css`), and this exact page uses it twice — once here, once in "Dalla campagna
al bicchiere" (confirmed via `grep -n` on `home.html`, not assumed). Scoped the new rule to

checking that the *other* button's own wrapper is a plain `.grids-area` with no `bottone-centro` class) so
only this one button moves, not the other. `-10px` is a reasonable "a bit" estimate, not measured.

**The button research question**: user asked where else `.bottone-home` appears and whether a site-wide
change to it would be low-risk. Investigated properly rather than answering from general impression:
`grep -rl "bottone-home"` across `src/content/main/` and `src/content/chrome/` returns only `home.html`,
`en/home.html`, `de/home.html` — this class is used **only on the homepage, and only twice** (both
instances found and quoted above), not on any other page in this rebuild. Also checked for other
similar-looking CTA buttons elsewhere on the site that might have been mistaken for the same one: found
none — the only other button-like class anywhere in `src/content/main/` is an unrelated Contact Form 7/UAGB
submit-button styler used solely on the contact page. Reported this finding directly to the user rather than
implementing anything for this part, since it was a question, not a request: a global CSS change to
`.bottone-home` would, in this rebuild's actual current state, only affect these exact 2 buttons — genuinely
low blast radius despite the "site-wide" framing, since the class simply isn't used anywhere else yet.
